========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it’s this business with bridges. I mean, I’ve been around a long time, driven plenty of places, and you’d think at my age I’d be past getting rattled by things. But if I know I have to cross a bridge—before I’m even on it—my heart starts pounding, and my hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest if I’m not the one driving.

It’s a terrible rush of fear, just all at once. Funny, I can sit and talk about other things—my old job, my grandchildren, whatever—but the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, that fear just takes over.

Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.

That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.

Walking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.

buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDoes thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.

I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.

  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.

I suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s this business with bridges. I mean, I’ve been around a long time, driven plenty of places, and you’d think at my age I’d be past getting rattled by things. But if I know I have to cross a bridge—before I’m even on it—my heart starts pounding, and my hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest if I’m not the one driving.\n\nIt’s a terrible rush of fear, just all at once. Funny, I can sit and talk about other things—my old job, my grandchildren, whatever—but the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, that fear just takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other types of situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s really the bridges—especially those high ones, the big spans. I know, rationally, thousands of cars go over them every day. I’m not foolish about that. But when I see one on the map, or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us.\n\nI suppose other things can make a person uneasy—my brother never liked elevators, funny enough—but for me, it’s that bridge feeling. That height, that span ahead. That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.

Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.\n\nI know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur when you are in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge, such as at the top of a tall building or a mountain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.

But if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s this business with bridges. I mean, I’ve been around a long time, driven plenty of places, and you’d think at my age I’d be past getting rattled by things. But if I know I have to cross a bridge—before I’m even on it—my heart starts pounding, and my hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest if I’m not the one driving.\n\nIt’s a terrible rush of fear, just all at once. Funny, I can sit and talk about other things—my old job, my grandchildren, whatever—but the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, that fear just takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other types of situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s really the bridges—especially those high ones, the big spans. I know, rationally, thousands of cars go over them every day. I’m not foolish about that. But when I see one on the map, or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us.\n\nI suppose other things can make a person uneasy—my brother never liked elevators, funny enough—but for me, it’s that bridge feeling. That height, that span ahead. That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.\n\nI know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur when you are in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge, such as at the top of a tall building or a mountain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine if fear is specific to bridges or also includes other elevated structures", "question": "Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a very high observation deck or platform that wasn't a bridge?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know, Doctor. I haven’t been on one of those high observation decks in a long time, if ever in the sort of way you mean.

My wife and I did go to places with views sometimes—she liked views, always did—but I tended to stay back a bit, look at the plaques or the gift shop, you know. They always have those little racks of postcards and magnets, and half the time the photograph is better than what you can see through the haze anyway.

So… would it be the same level of fear? I can’t say for certain. I imagine I wouldn’t be eager to stand right out on some platform and look down, but I don’t have a clear example like I do with bridges.

That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.\n\nI know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur when you are in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge, such as at the top of a tall building or a mountain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a very high observation deck or platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, Doctor. I haven’t been on one of those high observation decks in a long time, if ever in the sort of way you mean.\n\nMy wife and I did go to places with views sometimes—she liked views, always did—but I tended to stay back a bit, look at the plaques or the gift shop, you know. They always have those little racks of postcards and magnets, and half the time the photograph is better than what you can see through the haze anyway.\n\nSo… would it be the same level of fear? I can’t say for certain. I imagine I wouldn’t be eager to stand right out on some platform and look down, but I don’t have a clear example like I do with bridges.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not really sure about that one. Elevators… well, I’ve been in plenty of elevators, of course—doctor’s offices, hospitals, those parking garages where you can never remember whether you left the car on level C or level 3. My wife used to have a better memory for that sort of thing.

But a tall elevator, enclosed like that, I don’t know. If I can’t see out, I’m not sure it would strike me the same way. I suppose if somebody told me, “We’re going up fifty floors,” I might think about it, but I don’t have a clear memory of being frightened in that situation the way I do with certain bridges.

That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.\n\nI know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur when you are in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge, such as at the top of a tall building or a mountain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a very high observation deck or platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, Doctor. I haven’t been on one of those high observation decks in a long time, if ever in the sort of way you mean.\n\nMy wife and I did go to places with views sometimes—she liked views, always did—but I tended to stay back a bit, look at the plaques or the gift shop, you know. They always have those little racks of postcards and magnets, and half the time the photograph is better than what you can see through the haze anyway.\n\nSo… would it be the same level of fear? I can’t say for certain. I imagine I wouldn’t be eager to stand right out on some platform and look down, but I don’t have a clear example like I do with bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were in a very high, enclosed space like a tall elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. Elevators… well, I’ve been in plenty of elevators, of course—doctor’s offices, hospitals, those parking garages where you can never remember whether you left the car on level C or level 3. My wife used to have a better memory for that sort of thing.\n\nBut a tall elevator, enclosed like that, I don’t know. If I can’t see out, I’m not sure it would strike me the same way. I suppose if somebody told me, “We’re going up fifty floors,” I might think about it, but I don’t have a clear memory of being frightened in that situation the way I do with certain bridges.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "Determine if fear occurs in other high, enclosed spaces", "question": "Would you feel afraid in a very high, enclosed space like the top floor of a skyscraper with no outdoor access?"}

I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer on that. If it’s all enclosed, no balcony or outside bit, I might not even think about the height unless someone made a point of it.

I remember being in office buildings years ago—well, not skyscrapers exactly, more the kind with too many identical hallways and those fluorescent lights that make everyone look tired. My wife used to say I walked too fast in places like that, but I think I was just trying to find the right door before I forgot why we were there.

So, top floor with no outdoor access… I’m not sure. Maybe if there were huge windows and I could really see straight down, I might not like that. But I don’t have a definite example where I know I’d feel the same fear as on a bridge.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s this business with bridges. I mean, I’ve been around a long time, driven plenty of places, and you’d think at my age I’d be past getting rattled by things. But if I know I have to cross a bridge—before I’m even on it—my heart starts pounding, and my hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest if I’m not the one driving.\n\nIt’s a terrible rush of fear, just all at once. Funny, I can sit and talk about other things—my old job, my grandchildren, whatever—but the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, that fear just takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other types of situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s really the bridges—especially those high ones, the big spans. I know, rationally, thousands of cars go over them every day. I’m not foolish about that. But when I see one on the map, or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us.\n\nI suppose other things can make a person uneasy—my brother never liked elevators, funny enough—but for me, it’s that bridge feeling. That height, that span ahead. That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.\n\nI know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur when you are in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge, such as at the top of a tall building or a mountain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a very high observation deck or platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, Doctor. I haven’t been on one of those high observation decks in a long time, if ever in the sort of way you mean.\n\nMy wife and I did go to places with views sometimes—she liked views, always did—but I tended to stay back a bit, look at the plaques or the gift shop, you know. They always have those little racks of postcards and magnets, and half the time the photograph is better than what you can see through the haze anyway.\n\nSo… would it be the same level of fear? I can’t say for certain. I imagine I wouldn’t be eager to stand right out on some platform and look down, but I don’t have a clear example like I do with bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were in a very high, enclosed space like a tall elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. Elevators… well, I’ve been in plenty of elevators, of course—doctor’s offices, hospitals, those parking garages where you can never remember whether you left the car on level C or level 3. My wife used to have a better memory for that sort of thing.\n\nBut a tall elevator, enclosed like that, I don’t know. If I can’t see out, I’m not sure it would strike me the same way. I suppose if somebody told me, “We’re going up fifty floors,” I might think about it, but I don’t have a clear memory of being frightened in that situation the way I do with certain bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid in a very high, enclosed space like the top floor of a skyscraper with no outdoor access?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer on that. If it’s all enclosed, no balcony or outside bit, I might not even think about the height unless someone made a point of it.\n\nI remember being in office buildings years ago—well, not skyscrapers exactly, more the kind with too many identical hallways and those fluorescent lights that make everyone look tired. My wife used to say I walked too fast in places like that, but I think I was just trying to find the right door before I forgot why we were there.\n\nSo, top floor with no outdoor access… I’m not sure. Maybe if there were huge windows and I could really see straight down, I might not like that. But I don’t have a definite example where I know I’d feel the same fear as on a bridge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the bridge itself, I think—the crossing, yes, but especially when it’s one of those high spans. I can know in my head that thousands of cars go over it every day, trucks too, and nothing happens. I mean, they wouldn’t build the thing if it wasn’t meant to hold traffic.

But then I see it coming up through the windshield, or even notice it on the map ahead of time, and it feels… not reasonable, but it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Like the road won’t stay a road.

High places may be part of it, I suppose, but bridges have their own feeling to me. They’re different.

That height, that span ahead. That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not sure, doctor. A high narrow walkway with no handrail… that sounds unpleasant just hearing it, but I don’t know that I’ve actually been in that exact situation.

I remember once at a museum they had one of those little raised platforms—though it had rails, I think. Or maybe that was the train station, now that I’m thinking of it. My wife always liked reading all the signs, and I’d be halfway through the room before realizing she’d stopped behind me.

But no handrail, high up… I suppose I wouldn’t be eager to stand there. I just can’t say for certain whether it would hit me the same way a bridge does.

I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a very high observation deck or platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, Doctor. I haven’t been on one of those high observation decks in a long time, if ever in the sort of way you mean.\n\nMy wife and I did go to places with views sometimes—she liked views, always did—but I tended to stay back a bit, look at the plaques or the gift shop, you know. They always have those little racks of postcards and magnets, and half the time the photograph is better than what you can see through the haze anyway.\n\nSo… would it be the same level of fear? I can’t say for certain. I imagine I wouldn’t be eager to stand right out on some platform and look down, but I don’t have a clear example like I do with bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were in a very high, enclosed space like a tall elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. Elevators… well, I’ve been in plenty of elevators, of course—doctor’s offices, hospitals, those parking garages where you can never remember whether you left the car on level C or level 3. My wife used to have a better memory for that sort of thing.\n\nBut a tall elevator, enclosed like that, I don’t know. If I can’t see out, I’m not sure it would strike me the same way. I suppose if somebody told me, “We’re going up fifty floors,” I might think about it, but I don’t have a clear memory of being frightened in that situation the way I do with certain bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid in a very high, enclosed space like the top floor of a skyscraper with no outdoor access?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer on that. If it’s all enclosed, no balcony or outside bit, I might not even think about the height unless someone made a point of it.\n\nI remember being in office buildings years ago—well, not skyscrapers exactly, more the kind with too many identical hallways and those fluorescent lights that make everyone look tired. My wife used to say I walked too fast in places like that, but I think I was just trying to find the right door before I forgot why we were there.\n\nSo, top floor with no outdoor access… I’m not sure. Maybe if there were huge windows and I could really see straight down, I might not like that. But I don’t have a definite example where I know I’d feel the same fear as on a bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the act of crossing over the bridge, or is it primarily about being in a high place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the bridge itself, I think—the crossing, yes, but especially when it’s one of those high spans. I can know in my head that thousands of cars go over it every day, trucks too, and nothing happens. I mean, they wouldn’t build the thing if it wasn’t meant to hold traffic.\n\nBut then I see it coming up through the windshield, or even notice it on the map ahead of time, and it feels… not reasonable, but it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Like the road won’t stay a road.\n\nHigh places may be part of it, I suppose, but bridges have their own feeling to me. They’re different.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be triggered by standing on a high, narrow walkway with no handrail, even if it's not a bridge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor. A high narrow walkway with no handrail… that sounds unpleasant just hearing it, but I don’t know that I’ve actually been in that exact situation.\n\nI remember once at a museum they had one of those little raised platforms—though it had rails, I think. Or maybe that was the train station, now that I’m thinking of it. My wife always liked reading all the signs, and I’d be halfway through the room before realizing she’d stopped behind me.\n\nBut no handrail, high up… I suppose I wouldn’t be eager to stand there. I just can’t say for certain whether it would hit me the same way a bridge does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s the bridge itself, though the height makes it worse. It’s funny, I can sit here and know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day. They’re engineered for it, inspected, all that.

But then if I see a high span marked on the map, or it comes into view through the windshield, my mind doesn’t stay with all that sensible information. It’s like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Not every high place gives me that same feeling, but a bridge does.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s this business with bridges. I mean, I’ve been around a long time, driven plenty of places, and you’d think at my age I’d be past getting rattled by things. But if I know I have to cross a bridge—before I’m even on it—my heart starts pounding, and my hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest if I’m not the one driving.\n\nIt’s a terrible rush of fear, just all at once. Funny, I can sit and talk about other things—my old job, my grandchildren, whatever—but the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, that fear just takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other types of situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s really the bridges—especially those high ones, the big spans. I know, rationally, thousands of cars go over them every day. I’m not foolish about that. But when I see one on the map, or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us.\n\nI suppose other things can make a person uneasy—my brother never liked elevators, funny enough—but for me, it’s that bridge feeling. That height, that span ahead. That’s what sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur only when you are about to cross a bridge, or do you also experience it in other high places, such as tall buildings or mountain edges?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I’m not thrilled with heights in general, but it’s really the bridge that does it—the high span, you know. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, just like I know my grandson’s phone has more power than the computers they used to put men on the moon—he’s always telling me that—but when I see one coming up through the windshield, or even just notice it on a map, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away underneath us. That’s the part that grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a bridge, even from a distance or in a picture, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes, even seeing it from a distance can start it up—especially if it’s one of those high spans. A picture, or on a map, or coming up ahead through the windshield… I know full well cars go over those bridges all day long. I’m not saying it makes sense.\n\nBut in that moment it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to give way underneath us. Funny, my grandson showed me one of those satellite map things once—amazing what they can do now—but if I spot the bridge on there, I can feel myself tense up before I’m anywhere near it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when I’m the one driving, no. If the crossing becomes unavoidable—whether I’m behind the wheel or just sitting there being driven—my heart starts pounding before we’re even on it. My hands get damp, either on the steering wheel or gripping the armrest.\n\nWalking across… I try not to put myself in that position, to be honest. I remember years ago they used to talk about scenic walkways and all that, and my wife loved a view—she could stand there admiring the water like it was a postcard—but for me, once it’s clear I have to cross, that terrible rush of fear comes up right away. It’s the being committed to the bridge, I suppose, more than the particular way I’m crossing it.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about a bridge, without seeing it, trigger your fear?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it alone… yes, sometimes. Not every stray thought, mind you—I can think of the word “bridge” if we’re talking about cards or something, though I never learned bridge properly, my brother did—but if I picture one of those high spans, or remember it on a map, then it starts to feel real.\n\nI know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in my mind it’s like I can see it ahead through the windshield, and it feels as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear occur when you are in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge, such as at the top of a tall building or a mountain?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say, Doctor. I don’t really go up tall buildings much anymore, and mountains—well, my knees wouldn’t thank me for that, even if the rest of me wanted to.\n\nI suppose heights can make a person uneasy, can’t they? My wife used to love those lookout points, always wanted a photograph, and I’d be the one holding the camera crooked. But whether that’s the same fear as the bridge thing… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in the same clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar level of fear when you are in a high place but not moving, such as standing at the edge of a cliff or at the top of a tall building?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t spend much time at the edges of cliffs—at my age, even a curb can feel like something to pay attention to if the pavement’s uneven.\n\nTall buildings, I suppose I’ve been in them now and then, hospitals and offices and the like, but I don’t usually go right up to the glass and look down. My wife used to do that—she’d say, “Come see the view,” and I’d say I could see perfectly well from where I was. But whether that was the same kind of fear, the same level… I can’t say I’ve noticed it clearly. It’s not something I’ve tested much.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be less intense if you were on a very high bridge that did not span a body of water, such as a bridge over a wide valley?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that the water is the main thing, really. I mean, water makes it look worse sometimes—my wife used to say I shouldn’t stare down, which is easy advice to give when you’re not the one looking.\n\nBut if it’s a high span, even over a valley, I think it would still get me. I can know, in my sensible head, that cars go over those bridges all day long. But if I see it ahead of us, or even see it marked on the map and know we’re coming to it, it starts to feel like the whole bridge is just… waiting to drop away underneath. So no, I’m not sure it would be much less intense just because there wasn’t water.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a very high observation deck or platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, Doctor. I haven’t been on one of those high observation decks in a long time, if ever in the sort of way you mean.\n\nMy wife and I did go to places with views sometimes—she liked views, always did—but I tended to stay back a bit, look at the plaques or the gift shop, you know. They always have those little racks of postcards and magnets, and half the time the photograph is better than what you can see through the haze anyway.\n\nSo… would it be the same level of fear? I can’t say for certain. I imagine I wouldn’t be eager to stand right out on some platform and look down, but I don’t have a clear example like I do with bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were in a very high, enclosed space like a tall elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. Elevators… well, I’ve been in plenty of elevators, of course—doctor’s offices, hospitals, those parking garages where you can never remember whether you left the car on level C or level 3. My wife used to have a better memory for that sort of thing.\n\nBut a tall elevator, enclosed like that, I don’t know. If I can’t see out, I’m not sure it would strike me the same way. I suppose if somebody told me, “We’re going up fifty floors,” I might think about it, but I don’t have a clear memory of being frightened in that situation the way I do with certain bridges.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid in a very high, enclosed space like the top floor of a skyscraper with no outdoor access?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clean answer on that. If it’s all enclosed, no balcony or outside bit, I might not even think about the height unless someone made a point of it.\n\nI remember being in office buildings years ago—well, not skyscrapers exactly, more the kind with too many identical hallways and those fluorescent lights that make everyone look tired. My wife used to say I walked too fast in places like that, but I think I was just trying to find the right door before I forgot why we were there.\n\nSo, top floor with no outdoor access… I’m not sure. Maybe if there were huge windows and I could really see straight down, I might not like that. But I don’t have a definite example where I know I’d feel the same fear as on a bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the act of crossing over the bridge, or is it primarily about being in a high place?\n\nPatient: It’s more the bridge itself, I think—the crossing, yes, but especially when it’s one of those high spans. I can know in my head that thousands of cars go over it every day, trucks too, and nothing happens. I mean, they wouldn’t build the thing if it wasn’t meant to hold traffic.\n\nBut then I see it coming up through the windshield, or even notice it on the map ahead of time, and it feels… not reasonable, but it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Like the road won’t stay a road.\n\nHigh places may be part of it, I suppose, but bridges have their own feeling to me. They’re different.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be triggered by standing on a high, narrow walkway with no handrail, even if it's not a bridge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor. A high narrow walkway with no handrail… that sounds unpleasant just hearing it, but I don’t know that I’ve actually been in that exact situation.\n\nI remember once at a museum they had one of those little raised platforms—though it had rails, I think. Or maybe that was the train station, now that I’m thinking of it. My wife always liked reading all the signs, and I’d be halfway through the room before realizing she’d stopped behind me.\n\nBut no handrail, high up… I suppose I wouldn’t be eager to stand there. I just can’t say for certain whether it would hit me the same way a bridge does.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear is more about the height of the bridge or the fact that it's a bridge itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the bridge itself, though the height makes it worse. It’s funny, I can sit here and know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day. They’re engineered for it, inspected, all that.\n\nBut then if I see a high span marked on the map, or it comes into view through the windshield, my mind doesn’t stay with all that sensible information. It’s like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Not every high place gives me that same feeling, but a bridge does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

They’re engineered for it, inspected, all that.\n\nBut then if I see a high span marked on the map, or it comes into view through the windshield, my mind doesn’t stay with all that sensible information. It’s like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Not every high place gives me that same feeling, but a bridge does.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you feel afraid standing on a wide, flat, elevated surface with a secure fence or wall around it, such as a rooftop garden?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A rooftop garden… with a good fence around it? I don’t know that it would do the same thing to me. I mean, I might notice I’m up high, but that’s not quite the feeling.

With bridges, it’s different. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars go across them every day, but when I see one of those high spans coming up on the map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. A flat roof with a wall around it doesn’t quite have that same… road-over-empty-space feeling.
